        Let's recheck $k=3$ logic.
        $x = a_3$.
        $B_2$ terms are $a_4, a_5, a_6$.
        $a_4 = a_3 q = xq$.
        $a_5 = xq^2$.
        $a_6 = xq^3$.
        Sum $B_2 = xq + xq^2 + xq^3 = xq(1+q+q^2)$.
        $B_3$ terms $a_7, a_8, a_9$.
        $a_7 = a_3 q^4 = xq^4$.
        Sum $B_3 = xq^4(1+q+q^2)$.
        Ratio is $q^3$.
        Sums are $4$ and $6$.
        $q^3 = 1.5$.
        Why did I think $j=1$ gave 2?
        Ah, in my previous derivation for Case C ($k=3j$), I wrote:
        "Block $j+1$ sum $= 2(j+1)$ ... Block $j+2$ sum $= 2(j+2)$ ... $q^3 = \frac{j+2}{j+1}$".
        If $k=3j$, then the first term is $a_{3j}$.
        If $j=1$, $k=3$.
        The blocks fully contained are $B_{j+1}$?
        Range $[3j, 3j+8]$.
        $B_1$: $1..3$.
        $B_2$: $4..6$.
        $B_3$: $7..9$.
        $B_4$: $10..12$.
        For $k=3$ ($j=1$):
        Range $[3, 11]$.
        $B_1$ is $1..3$. Intersects $[3, 3]$. Partial.
        $B_2$ is $4..6$. Contained.
        $B_3$ is $7..9$. Contained.
        $B_4$ is $10..12$. Intersects $[10, 11]$. Partial.
        So full blocks are $B_2$ and $B_3$.
        Indices of full blocks are $j+1$ and $j+2$.
        Sum $B_2 = 2(2) = 4$.
        Sum $B_3 = 2(3) = 6$.
        Ratio $6/4 = 1.5$.
        So $q^3 = 1.5$.
        Wait, what about $B_1$?
        $B_1$ sum is 2.
        Is there a constraint from $B_1$?
        GP terms in $B_1$: just $a_3$.
        $a_3 = x$.
        $B_1$ sum $= a_1 + a_2 + x = 2$.
        This allows finding $a_1, a_2$. No constraint on $x, q$ other than real.
        So $q^3 = 1.5$ is the constraint derived from full blocks.
        
        Let's check if we can shift the "full blocks" to have different sums.
        The GP length is 9.
        It can cover:
        1. 3 full blocks (if $k \equiv 1$). Implies $q^3 = S_{n+1}/S_n$ and $S_{n+2}/S_{n+1}$.
           $S_n = 2n$.
           $q^3 = \frac{2(n+1)}{2n} = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
           Also $q^3 = \frac{n+2}{n+1}$.
           Impossible.
        
        2. 2 full blocks (if $k \equiv 2$ or $k \equiv 0$).
           If $k \equiv 2$:
           Start at $k$.
           $B_{p}$ (start block) partial.
           $B_{p+1}$ full.
           $B_{p+2}$ full.
           $B_{p+3}$ (end block) partial.
           Here $p = \lceil k/3 \rceil$.
           If $k=2$, $p=1$. Full blocks $B_2, B_3$. Sums $4, 6$. Ratio $1.5$.
           If $k=5$, $p=2$. Full blocks $B_3, B_4$. Sums $6, 8$. Ratio $8/6 = 4/3$.
           Generally, if full blocks are $B_m, B_{m+1}$, then $q^3 = \frac{2(m+1)}{2m} = \frac{m+1}{m} = 1 + 1/m$.
           To maximize $q$, we need smallest $m$.
           For $k \equiv 2$, $k=3m'+2$.
           The first full block is $B_{m'+2}$?
           Let's trace.
           $k=2 \implies m'=0$. Full blocks start at $B_2$. So $m=2$.
           $k=5 \implies m'=1$. Full blocks start at $B_3$. So $m=3$.
           Wait, for $k=2$, range $[2, 10]$. $B_1$ partial, $B_2, B_3$ full, $B_4$ partial.
           So $m=2$.
           $q^3 = \frac{2(3)}{2(2)} = 1.5$.
           For $k=5$, range $[5, 13]$.
           $B_2$ ($4..6$) partial.
           $B_3$ ($7..9$) full.
           $B_4$ ($10..12$) full.
           $B_5$ ($13..15$) partial.
           So $m=3$. $q^3 = 4/3$.
           So for $k \equiv 2$, $m \ge 2$. Max $q^3 = 1.5$.
           
           What if $k \equiv 0$?
           $k=3$. Range $[3, 11]$.
           $B_1$ partial.
           $B_2$ full.
           $B_3$ full.
           $B_4$ partial.
           So $m=2$. $q^3 = 1.5$.
           $k=6$. Range $[6, 14]$.
           $B_2$ partial.
           $B_3$ full.
           $B_4$ full.
           $B_5$ partial.
           $m=3$. $q^3 = 4/3$.
           So for $k \equiv 0$, $m \ge 2$. Max $q^3 = 1.5$.
        
        3. 1 full block?
           Is it possible to have only 1 full block?
           Length 9. Block size 3.
           If start at $k$, end at $k+8$.
           To have only 1 full block, we need the range to "straddle" boundaries such that it catches only one full 3-segment.
           The maximum span not covering 2 full blocks would be length $< 2 \times 3 + 2 = 8$?
           Let's check.
           Worst case to minimize full blocks: start just after a block ends, end just before a block ends?
           No, we want to check if $m$ can be 1.
           Full block $B_1$ is $1..3$.
           Can the GP cover only $B_1$ and parts of neighbors?
           Range length 9.
           If it covers $B_1$, it covers indices $\le 3$.
           To avoid covering $B_2$ ($4..6$), it must end at $\le 5$? No, must not cover all of $B_2$.
           But length is 9.
           Indices $k, \dots, k+8$.
           If $k=1$, covers $1..9$. $B_1, B_2, B_3$ full.
           If $k=2$, covers $2..10$. $B_2, B_3$ full. ($B_1$ not full).
           If $k=3$, covers $3..11$. $B_2, B_3$ full.
           If $k=4$, covers $4..12$. $B_2, B_3, B_4$ full.
           Wait.
           $B_2$ is $4..6$. $B_3$ is $7..9$. $B_4$ is $10..12$.
           Range $[4, 12]$ contains $4..6, 7..9, 10..12$.
           So $k=4$ (which is $k \equiv 1$) gives 3 full blocks.
           Generally, if $k \equiv 1$, we get 3 full blocks $B_{p}, B_{p+1}, B_{p+2}$.
           If $k \equiv 2$, we get 2 full blocks $B_{p+1}, B_{p+2}$.
           If $k \equiv 0$, we get 2 full blocks $B_{p+1}, B_{p+2}$?
           Let's check $k=3$.
           $B_1$ ($1..3$) -> contains 3. Partial.
           $B_2$ ($4..6$) -> contained.
           $B_3$ ($7..9$) -> contained.
           $B_4$ ($10..12$) -> contains $10, 11$. Partial.
           Yes, 2 full blocks.
           Can we have fewer?
           What if $k$ is large?
           If $k=1$, full blocks $1, 2, 3$.
           If $k=2$, full blocks $2, 3$.
           If $k=3$, full blocks $2, 3$.
           If $k=4$, full blocks $2, 3, 4$.
           If $k=5$, full blocks $3, 4$.
           If $k=6$, full blocks $3, 4$.
           So it's always 2 or 3 full blocks.
           With 3 full blocks ($k \equiv 1$), we found a contradiction ($q^3$ must satisfy two different ratios).
           So only cases with 2 full blocks are viable.
           These are $k \equiv 2$ and $k \equiv 0$.
           In these cases, the full blocks are $B_{m}$ and $B_{m+1}$ for some $m$.
           The ratio is $q^3 = \frac{2(m+1)}{2m} = \frac{m+1}{m}$.
           We need to determine the possible values of $m$.
           
           For $k \equiv 2$: $k=2, 5, 8, \dots$.
           $k=2$: Range $[2, 10]$. Full $B_2, B_3$. So $m=2$. Ratio $3/2$.
           $k=5$: Range $[5, 13]$. Full $B_3, B_4$. So $m=3$. Ratio $4/3$.
           $k=3j+2$. Full blocks start at $j+2$. So $m = j+2$.
           Since $k \ge 1$, $j \ge 0$. So $m \ge 2$.
           
           For $k \equiv 0$: $k=3, 6, 9, \dots$.
           $k=3$: Range $[3, 11]$. Full $B_2, B_3$. So $m=2$. Ratio $3/2$.
           $k=6$: Range $[6, 14]$. Full $B_3, B_4$. So $m=3$. Ratio $4/3$.
           $k=3j$. Full blocks start at $j+1$. So $m = j+1$.
           Since $k \ge 1 \implies j \ge 1$. So $m \ge 2$.
           
           So in all valid cases, $m \ge 2$.
           Thus $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m} = 1 + \frac{1}{m} \le 1 + \frac{1}{2} = 1.5$.
           So max $q^3 = 1.5$.
           Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    **Wait, is there any other possibility?**
    I assumed that if a block is not fully contained, it imposes no constraint on $q$ other than existence of terms.
    Let's verify this.
    In Case $k \equiv 2$ with $k=2$ (so $m=2$), we have full blocks $B_2, B_3$.
    $B_1$ contains $a_2, a_3$. $a_1$ is free.
    $B_4$ contains $a_{10}$. $a_{11}, a_{12}$ are free.
    Constraints:
    $a_2 = x$.
    $a_3 = xq$.
    $a_4 = xq^2, a_5 = xq^3, a_6 = xq^4$.
    $a_7 = xq^5, a_8 = xq^6, a_9 = xq^7$.
    $a_{10} = xq^8$.
    
    Block sums:
    $B_1$: $a_1 + x + xq = 2$. $\implies a_1 = 2 - x(1+q)$.
    $B_2$: $xq^2 + xq^3 + xq^4 = 4$. $\implies xq^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $B_3$: $xq^5 + xq^6 + xq^7 = 6$. $\implies xq^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    $B_4$: $xq^8 + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    
    From $B_2, B_3$, we get $q^3 = 1.5$.
    Then $x$ is determined by $B_2$ sum.
    $x = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    Then $a_1$ is determined.
    Then $a_{11} + a_{12} = 8 - xq^8$. We can choose $a_{11}, a_{12}$.
    So this works.
    
    What if $m=1$?
    This would require full blocks to be $B_1, B_2$.
    Sums $2, 4$. Ratio $2$.
    This implies $q^3 = 2$.
    Can we have full blocks $B_1, B_2$?
    This requires the GP range to fully contain $B_1$ and $B_2$.
    $B_1$ is $1..3$. $B_2$ is $4..6$.
    So range must include $[1, 6]$.
    GP range is $[k, k+8]$.
    So $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 6$.
    $k \le 1 \implies k=1$.
    If $k=1$, range is $[1, 9]$.
    This includes $B_1, B_2, B_3$.
    Full blocks are $B_1, B_2, B_3$.
    Sums $2, 4, 6$.
    This leads to the contradiction $q^3 = 2$ and $q^3 = 1.5$.
    So we cannot have *only* $B_1, B_2$ as full blocks if $B_3$ is also full.
    However, what if $B_3$ is NOT full?
    Range $[1, 9]$ definitely contains $B_3$ ($7..9$).
    So if $k=1$, $B_3$ is full.
    Is there any $k$ such that $B_1, B_2$ are full but $B_3$ is not?
    We need $[k, k+8] \supseteq [1, 6]$.
    And $[k, k+8] \not\supseteq [7, 9]$.
    So $k+8 < 7 \implies k < -1$. Impossible.
    Or $[k, k+8]$ starts after 7? No, must cover 1.
    So if $B_1$ is full, $k \le 1$.
    If $B_2$ is full, $k+8 \ge 6 \implies k \ge -2$.
    If $k=1$, $B_3$ is full.
    So we can't avoid $B_3$ being full if $B_1, B_2$ are full.
    
    Wait, what if $B_1$ is NOT full?
    If we want $q^3 = 2$, we need ratio of sums to be 2.
    This happens if we compare sums of $B_1$ and $B_2$.
    So we need $B_1$ and $B_2$ to be "effective" in determining $q$.
    If they are full blocks, their sums are fixed.
    If they are not full blocks, do they constrain $q$?
    Suppose $B_1$ is not full. Then it contains partial terms.
    $B_1$ sum $= a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
    If GP terms are in $B_1$, say $a_2, a_3$ (Case $k=2$).
    $a_1$ is free.
    $a_2 + a_3 = x + xq$.
    $a_1 = 2 - x(1+q)$.
    This does not constrain $x, q$ relation directly, other than $a_1$ exists.
    The constraint comes from blocks where the GP terms sum up to the FULL block sum without free variables to adjust.
    That happens when the GP covers the whole block.
    So, if we want to achieve $q^3 = 2$, we need to find a scenario where we can enforce a ratio of 2 between some block sums using the GP terms.
    This requires two blocks to be fully covered by the GP.
    Let these be $B_i$ and $B_j$.
    Then $q^{3(j-i)} = \frac{2j}{2i} = \frac{j}{i}$.
    Since GP length is 9, the number of terms is limited.
    The indices of full blocks must be within range $[k, k+8]$.
    Let full blocks be $B_{p}, B_{p+1}, \dots, B_{p+r}$.
    Then $q^3 = \frac{p+1}{p} = \frac{p+2}{p+1} = \dots$.
    This requires $\frac{p+1}{p} = \frac{p+2}{p+1} \implies (p+1)^2 = p(p+2) \implies p^2+2p+1 = p^2+2p \implies 1=0$ impossible.
    So we can have at most one ratio constraint from consecutive full blocks.
    Actually, if we have $r \ge 2$ full blocks, we have constraints $q^3 = \frac{p+1}{p}$ and $q^3 = \frac{p+2}{p+1}$, which is impossible.
    Therefore, we can have at most 1 "interval" of full blocks where the ratio is consistent?
    Wait, if we have 3 full blocks $B_1, B_2, B_3$, we need $q^3 = 2$ AND $q^3 = 1.5$. Impossible.
    So we CANNOT have 3 full blocks.
    This confirms $k \equiv 1$ is impossible.
    
    So we must have exactly 2 full blocks.
    Let them be $B_m, B_{m+1}$.
    Then $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$.
    We need to check if it's possible to have exactly $B_m, B_{m+1}$ full.
    For $k \equiv 2$: Full blocks are $B_{m'+2}, B_{m'+3}$ where $k=3m'+2$.
    Let $m = m'+2$. Since $m' \ge 0$, $m \ge 2$.
    So $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ with $m \ge 2$.
    Max $q^3 = 3/2$.
    
    For $k \equiv 0$: Full blocks are $B_{j+1}, B_{j+2}$ where $k=3j$.
    Let $m = j+1$. Since $j \ge 1$, $m \ge 2$.
    So $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ with $m \ge 2$.
    Max $q^3 = 3/2$.
    
    So it seems $q^3 = 1.5$ is the maximum.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    
    **Is there any loophole?**
    What if $q$ is negative?
    "q的最大值" implies we want the largest real $q$.
    If $q$ is negative, $q^3$ is negative.
    Sums of blocks are positive ($2n$).
    Sum of GP segment in a block is $x q^{start} (1+q+q^2)$.
    $1+q+q^2$ is always positive for real $q$ (min value $3/4$).
    $x$ can be negative.
    If $x < 0$, then sum is negative?
    If $x < 0$ and $q < 0$.
    Let's check signs.
    $S_{block} = 2n > 0$.
    If block is fully covered by GP, Sum $= x q^A (1+q+q^2)$.
    Since $1+q+q^2 > 0$, we need $x q^A > 0$.
    If $q < 0$, $q^A$ alternates sign.
    So $x$ must alternate sign or be such that $x q^A > 0$.
    If $x > 0$, then $A$ must be even.
    If $x < 0$, then $A$ must be odd.
    This is just a constraint on $x$.
    Does this allow higher $q$?
    We are looking for max $q$. Positive $q$ are generally larger than negative $q$.
    If $q > 0$, $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$. Max $1.5$.
    If $q < 0$, let $q = -p$ with $p > 0$.
    $q^3 = -p^3$.
    $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ is positive.
    So we cannot have $q < 0$ if we rely on the full block ratio argument derived from $q^3$.
    Wait, the derivation $q^3 = \frac{Sum_{B_{m+1}}}{Sum_{B_m}}$ assumed:
    $Sum_{B_m} = C q^{something}$.
    $Sum_{B_{m+1}} = C q^{something+3}$.
    So $Sum_{B_{m+1}} / Sum_{B_m} = q^3$.
    Since sums are positive ($2m, 2(m+1)$), the ratio is positive.
    Thus $q^3 > 0$, so $q > 0$.
    So $q$ must be positive.
    So max $q$ comes from max $q^3$.
    
    **Is it possible that the blocks are not consecutive?**
    No, the GP is contiguous. The blocks are contiguous segments of indices.
    The blocks fully contained in the GP range must be consecutive.
    
    **Are there cases with NO full blocks?**
    If no block is fully contained, then we don't have the constraint $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ derived from fixed sums.
    However, if no block is fully contained, does that mean $q$ is unconstrained?
    Let's check.
    If no block is fully contained, it means for every block $B_n$, the GP range $[k, k+8]$ does not contain $[3n-2, 3n]$.
    This means for every $n$, either $k > 3n-2$ (start after block) or $k+8 < 3n$ (end before block).
    Basically, the GP range "fits in the gaps" or overlaps boundaries such that no full 3-segment is captured.
    Let's analyze the "gaps".
    Blocks are $1..3, 4..6, 7..9 \dots$.
    The "gaps" between blocks are just the boundaries.
    But blocks cover everything.
    The only way to avoid a full block is to be "short" or "aligned" such that you miss at least one element of every block.
    Block length is 3.
    To miss at least 1 element from $B_1$ ($1,2,3$), we must exclude 1, or 2, or 3.
    To miss at least 1 from $B_2$ ($4,5,6$), exclude 4, 5, or 6.
    GP length is 9.
    Can we construct a range of length 9 that hits at most 2 elements of every block?
    Let's try.
    Blocks $B_1, B_2, B_3, B_4$.
    $B_1: 1, 2, 3$.
    $B_2: 4, 5, 6$.
    $B_3: 7, 8, 9$.
    $B_4: 10, 11, 12$.
    We need to pick a range of 9 integers.
    If we pick $2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10$.
    $B_1$: contains $2, 3$. Misses 1. OK.
    $B_2$: contains $4, 5, 6$. Full. Not OK.
    We want to avoid full blocks.
    We need to break every block of 3.
    This implies the range cannot contain any $i, i+1, i+2$ where $i \equiv 1 \pmod 3$.
    So we cannot contain $1,2,3$ or $4,5,6$ or $7,8,9$ or $10,11,12$.
    This means the set of indices $I \subset \mathbb{Z}$ with $|I|=9$ (consecutive) must not contain any set $\{3m-2, 3m-1, 3m\}$.
    This is equivalent to saying the consecutive integers do not cover a full set of residues mod 3?
    No, residues are $1, 2, 0$.
    Any sequence of 3 consecutive integers covers all residues mod 3.
    Specifically, it covers a full block $B_m$ if the indices match.
    Indices are $n$.
    $n \in [k, k+8]$.
    $B_m$ is $\{3m-2, 3m-1, 3m\}$.
    This set is fully contained in $[k, k+8]$ iff $k \le 3m-2$ and $3m \le k+8$.
    $3m-2 \ge k \implies m \ge \lceil (k+2)/3 \rceil$.
    $3m \le k+8 \implies m \le \lfloor (k+8)/3 \rfloor$.
    So full blocks exist if there is an integer $m$ in that range.
    The number of integers is $\lfloor \frac{k+8}{3} \rfloor - \lceil \frac{k+2}{3} \rceil + 1$.
    Let's approximate: $\frac{k+8}{3} - \frac{k+2}{3} + 1 = \frac{6}{3} + 1 = 3$.
    So there are roughly 3 full blocks.
    Exact calculation:
    Let $k = 3j + r$, $r \in \{1, 2, 0\}$. (Using $1,2,3$ or $1,2,0$).
    Let's use $k = 3j + 1, 3j + 2, 3j + 3$.
    
    Case $k = 3j + 1$:
    Lower bound for $m$: $\lceil \frac{3j+3}{3} \rceil = j+1$.
    Upper bound: $\lfloor \frac{3j+9}{3} \rfloor = j+3$.
    $m \in \{j+1, j+2, j+3\}$.
    So 3 full blocks: $B_{j+1}, B_{j+2}, B_{j+3}$.
    (Matches previous analysis).
    
    Case $k = 3j + 2$:
    Lower: $\lceil \frac{3j+4}{3} \rceil = j+2$.
    Upper: $\lfloor \frac{3j+10}{3} \rfloor = j+3$.
    $m \in \{j+2, j+3\}$.
    So 2 full blocks: $B_{j+2}, B_{j+3}$.
    (Matches previous).
    
    Case $k = 3j + 3$ (i.e., $3(j+1)$):
    Lower: $\lceil \frac{3j+5}{3} \rceil = j+2$.
    Upper: $\lfloor \frac{3j+11}{3} \rfloor = j+3$.
    $m \in \{j+2, j+3\}$.
    So 2 full blocks: $B_{j+2}, B_{j+3}$.
    (Matches previous).
    
    So it is impossible to have 0 full blocks.
    Minimum number of full blocks is 2.
    Maximum is 3.
    With 3 full blocks, we established contradiction.
    With 2 full blocks, we have $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ where $m$ is the index of the first full block.
    For $k=3j+2$, $m = j+2$. Min $j=0 \implies k=2 \implies m=2$.
    For $k=3j+3$, $m = j+2$. Min $j=0 \implies k=3 \implies m=2$.
    Wait, $k$ must be $\ge 1$.
    If $k=3$, $j=0$ in $3j+3$ form?
    If we write $k=3j$, $j \ge 1$. Then $m = j+1$. Min $m=2$.
    If we write $k=3j+2$, $j \ge 0$. Then $m = j+2$. Min $m=2$.
    So in all valid cases, $m \ge 2$.
    Thus $q^3 \le 1.5$.
    $q \le \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    
    **Is it possible to have non-consecutive full blocks?**
    No, indices are consecutive.
    
    **Double Check the "No Contradiction" logic.**
    For $k=2$ ($m=2$), we have full blocks $B_2, B_3$.
    Sums 4, 6.
    $q^3 = 1.5$.
    Are there any other constraints?
    We checked $B_1$ and $B_4$ can be satisfied.
    Is there a global constraint?
    $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    This is the sum of first $3n$ terms.
    This is satisfied if $B_i$ sums to $2i$.
    We constructed the sequence such that $B_i$ sums are correct.
    Are there constraints on $a_n$ being real? Yes, we ensured $x, u, v$ real.
    Is there any constraint on $a_n$?
    "Exists a sequence".
    Are there any implicit constraints?
    Usually not.
    
    Wait, what if $1+q+q^2 = 0$?
    Then sum of any full block covered by GP would be 0.
    But sums are $2n \ne 0$.
    So $1+q+q^2 \ne 0$.
    Also $x \ne 0$.
    If $x=0$, all GP terms 0.
    Then sums of full blocks covered by GP would be 0.
    But sums are $2n \ne 0$.
    So $x \ne 0$.
    So the derivation holds.
    
    Let's check if $q$ can be larger if $m$ can be 1.
    $m=1$ implies $B_1, B_2$ are full blocks.
    This requires range $[k, k+8]$ to contain $[1, 6]$.
    So $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 6$.
    $k \le 1$. Since $k \in \mathbb{N}^*$, $k=1$.
    If $k=1$, range is $[1, 9]$.
    This contains $[1, 6]$ and also $[7, 9]$.
    So $B_3$ is also full.
    So we have $B_1, B_2, B_3$ full.
    Sums $2, 4, 6$.
    GP sums $S_1, S_2, S_3$.
    $S_2/S_1 = q^3 = 4/2 = 2$.

    Let's write down the argument clearly.

        - If 3 blocks $B_{m-1}, B_m, B_{m+1}$ full, ratio $q^3 = \frac{m}{m-1}$ and $\frac{m+1}{m}$, impossible.
        - If 3 blocks $B_m, B_{m+1}, B_{m+2}$ full, ratio $q^3 = \frac{m+1}{m}$ and $\frac{m+2}{m+1}$, impossible.

    11. We found that $m=1$ requires $k \le 0$, impossible.
    12. So $m \ge 2$.
    13. $q^3 = 1 + 1/m$ is decreasing for $m \ge 1$.
    14. Max $q^3$ is at $m=2$, value $1.5$.
